From the source, this section takes the order of ideas: the cost of Newton’s method, the secant condition, and the BFGS and limited-memory BFGS updates. The source states the updates as solutions of nearest-matrix problems without proof; here the BFGS update is written out. The Davidon-Fletcher-Powell and rank-1 updates, the nearest-matrix derivation, the history, and the Julia code are not part of this section.

DefDefinition 41 (Secant condition)

Definition 41 (Secant condition) Let \(f : \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}\) have first partial derivatives, and let \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}, \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+ \in \mathbb{R}^p\). Write \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} \stackrel{\text{def}}{=}\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+ - \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}\) for the step and \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}\stackrel{\text{def}}{=}\frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+) - \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}})\) for the change in the gradient. A symmetric matrix \(\mathbf{B}\) satisfies the secant condition [23, Ch. 6] for \((\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}, \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}})\) when

\[ \mathbf{B} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} = \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}. \]

ExmExample 59 (The Hessian of a quadratic function satisfies the secant condition)

Example 59 (The Hessian of a quadratic function satisfies the secant condition) Let \(f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}) = \tfrac{1}{2} {\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}}^{\top} \mathbf{Q} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}} - \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{b}}\vphantom{b}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}\) with \(\mathbf{Q}\) symmetric. Then \(\frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}) = \mathbf{Q} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}- \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{b}}\vphantom{b}}\), so for any \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}\) and \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+\)

\[ \begin{aligned} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} &= (\mathbf{Q} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+ - \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{b}}\vphantom{b}}) - (\mathbf{Q} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}- \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{b}}\vphantom{b}}) && \text{(gradient at each point)} \\ &= \mathbf{Q} (\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+ - \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}) && \text{(collect terms)} \\ &= \mathbf{Q} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}. && \text{(definition of } \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} \text{)} \end{aligned} \]

So the Hessian \(\mathbf{Q}\) of the quadratic function satisfies the secant condition for every step. For a general \(f\), \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}\approx \mathbf{H}_f \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}\) holds for a short step, with \(\mathbf{H}_f\) the Hessian of \(f\) near the step, which is why a matrix that satisfies the secant condition is a reasonable stand-in for the Hessian.

DefDefinition 42 (Quasi-Newton method)

Definition 42 (Quasi-Newton method) Let \(f : \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}\) have first partial derivatives, let \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(0)}\) be a starting point, and let \(\mathbf{H}^{(0)}\) be a positive definite matrix. A quasi-Newton method [23, Ch. 6] produces, for \(t = 0, 1, 2, \ldots\),

\[ \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(t+1)} \stackrel{\text{def}}{=}\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(t)} - \alpha^{(t)} \mathbf{H}^{(t)} \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(t)}), \]

where \(\alpha^{(t)} > 0\) is a step length chosen by a line search, and \(\mathbf{H}^{(t+1)}\) is a symmetric positive definite matrix whose inverse satisfies the secant condition, that is, \(\mathbf{H}^{(t+1)} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}^{(t)} = \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}^{(t)}\), with \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}^{(t)} = \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(t+1)} - \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(t)}\) and \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}^{(t)} = \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(t+1)}) - \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(t)})\).

ExmExample 60 (One quasi-Newton step on a quadratic function)

Example 60 (One quasi-Newton step on a quadratic function) Take \(f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}) = \tfrac{1}{2} {\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}}^{\top} \mathbf{Q} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}} - \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{b}}\vphantom{b}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}\) with \(\mathbf{Q} = \begin{bmatrix} 4 & 1 \\ 1 & 3 \end{bmatrix}\) and \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{b}}\vphantom{b}} = {(1, 2)}^{\top}\) (Example 59), \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(0)} = \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{0}}\vphantom{0}}\) and \(\mathbf{H}^{(0)} = \mathbf{I}_2\). For a quadratic function, the step length that minimizes \(f\) along a direction \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{d}}\vphantom{d}}\) is \(\alpha = -\frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}) \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{d}}\vphantom{d}} / {\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{d}}\vphantom{d}}}^{\top} \mathbf{Q} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{d}}\vphantom{d}}\).

Q <- matrix(c(4, 1, 1, 3), nrow = 2)
b <- c(1, 2)
f <- function(x) 0.5 * sum(x * (Q %*% x)) - sum(b * x)
grad <- function(x) drop(Q %*% x - b)
x0 <- c(0, 0)
H0 <- diag(2)
d <- -drop(H0 %*% grad(x0))
step <- -sum(grad(x0) * d) / drop(t(d) %*% Q %*% d)
x1 <- x0 + step * d
s <- x1 - x0
y <- grad(x1) - grad(x0)

The step length is 0.25, which moves the iterate to \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(1)} = (0.25, 0.5)\) and lowers \(f\) from 0 to -0.625. The difference of the gradients, \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}^{(0)} = (1.5, 1.75)\), is \(\mathbf{Q} \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}^{(0)}\) (the secant condition for a quadratic function).

RemarkRemark 5 (Why a quasi-Newton method is used)

Remark 5 (Why a quasi-Newton method is used). Because \(\mathbf{H}^{(t)}\) is positive definite, \(-\mathbf{H}^{(t)} \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(t)})\) is a descent direction, so, when the gradient is not \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{0}}\vphantom{0}}\), a line search finds a step that decreases \(f\). Newton’s method needs the Hessian and the solution of a system of equations at every iteration, which costs about \(p^3\) operations for a dense \(p \times p\) Hessian, and requires the formulas for its entries. A quasi-Newton method needs only the gradient and an update of \(\mathbf{H}^{(t)}\) costing about \(p^2\) operations. Near a minimizer the method speeds up superlinearly: faster than gradient descent, slower than Newton’s method [23, Ch. 6]. Replacing \(\mathbf{H}^{(t)}\) by \(\mathbf{I}_p\) in the step formula gives gradient descent.

DefDefinition 43 (BFGS update (Broyden-Fletcher-Goldfarb-Shanno))

Definition 43 (BFGS update (Broyden-Fletcher-Goldfarb-Shanno)) Let \(\mathbf{H}\), an approximation of the inverse Hessian, be a \(p \times p\) positive definite matrix, and let \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}, \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}\in \mathbb{R}^p\) satisfy \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} > 0\). With \(\rho \stackrel{\text{def}}{=}1 / \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}\), the BFGS update [23, Ch. 6] of \(\mathbf{H}\) is

\[ \mathbf{H}^+ \stackrel{\text{def}}{=}\mathopen{}\left(\mathbf{I}_p - \rho \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} {\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}}^{\top}\right)\mathclose{} \mathbf{H} \mathopen{}\left(\mathbf{I}_p - \rho \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} {\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}}^{\top}\right)\mathclose{} + \rho \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} {\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}}^{\top}. \]

ExmExample 61 (A BFGS update from the quasi-Newton step)

Example 61 (A BFGS update from the quasi-Newton step) Continue Example 60 with \(\mathbf{H} = \mathbf{I}_2\), \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} = \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}^{(0)}\), and \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}= \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}^{(0)}\).

rho <- 1 / sum(y * s)
left <- diag(2) - rho * s %*% t(y)
H1 <- left %*% H0 %*% t(left) + rho * s %*% t(s)
H1
#>         [,1]   [,2]
#> [1,]  0.6625 -0.425
#> [2,] -0.4250  0.650
drop(H1 %*% y)
#> [1] 0.25 0.50

Here \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} = 1.25 > 0\), so \(\rho = 0.8\). The updated matrix satisfies \(\mathbf{H}^+ \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}= (0.25, 0.5)\), which is \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} = (0.25, 0.5)\), and its smallest eigenvalue is 0.231204, so \(\mathbf{H}^+\) is positive definite.

RemarkRemark 6 (The curvature condition \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} > 0\))

Remark 6 (The curvature condition \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} > 0\)). If the strict inequality of Theorem 10 holds for distinct points, then \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} > 0\) for \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} = \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+ - \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}\ne \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{0}}\vphantom{0}}\) and \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}= \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+) - \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}})\). Add the versions at \((\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+, \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}})\) and at \((\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}, \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+)\):

\[ f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+) + f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}) > f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}) + f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+) + \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}) \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} - \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^+) \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}, \]

so \(0 > -\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}\). For a general \(f\), a line search that enforces a curvature condition on the step length (the Wolfe conditions [23, Ch. 3]) guarantees \(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}} \cdot \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}} > 0\). Such a step can be found along a descent direction when \(f\) cannot keep decreasing forever.

DefDefinition 44 (Limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS))

Definition 44 (Limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS)) Limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) [23, Ch. 7] is the quasi-Newton method that never forms \(\mathbf{H}^{(t)}\). It stores only the \(m\) most recent pairs \((\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}^{(j)}, \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}^{(j)})\), with \(m\) small, such as \(5\) or \(20\), and obtains \(\mathbf{H}^{(t)} \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(t)})\) implicitly, through a few vector operations, as if the BFGS update for those \(m\) pairs were applied to a simple starting matrix, such as a multiple of \(\mathbf{I}_p\).

ExmExample 62 (L-BFGS applies the update without forming the matrix)

Example 62 (L-BFGS applies the update without forming the matrix) Continue Example 61 with \(m = 1\) stored pair \((\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{s}}\vphantom{s}}^{(0)}, \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{y}}\vphantom{y}}^{(0)})\) and starting matrix \(\mathbf{I}_2\). Below, \(\mathbf{H}^+ \frac{\partial}{\partial \mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}} f(\mathord{\smash[b]{\underset{\raise.3ex\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}{x}}\vphantom{x}}^{(1)})\) is formed from vectors alone, starting from H0, then compared with the matrix product.

g1 <- grad(x1)
alpha <- rho * sum(s * g1)
q <- g1 - alpha * y
r <- drop(H0 %*% q)
beta <- rho * sum(y * r)
r <- r + s * (alpha - beta)
rbind(from_vectors = r, from_matrix = drop(H1 %*% g1))
#>                [,1]   [,2]
#> from_vectors 0.4375 -0.375
#> from_matrix  0.4375 -0.375

The two rows agree to within 5.6e-17, although the first never forms the matrix. BFGS stores a dense \(p \times p\) matrix and spends about \(p^2\) operations per iteration; L-BFGS stores \(2m\) vectors with \(p\) entries and spends about \(m p\) operations per iteration, so it remains usable when \(p\) is in the millions.

7 Further reading

  • Boyd and Vandenberghe [24] is the standard reference on convex optimization, covering convex sets, convex functions, convex optimization problems, duality, unconstrained minimization, and interior-point methods.
  • Nocedal and Wright [23] is a comprehensive reference on continuous numerical optimization, covering line search methods, trust-region methods, conjugate gradient methods, quasi-Newton methods, and nonlinear equations.
  • Hua Zhou’s course page for “UCLA Biostat 257 - Statistical Computing” (2025 Spring) has lectures on optimization, from root finding to constrained problems. Its source is on GitHub under the MIT License.
  • Hua Zhou’s lecture notes for “UCLA Biostat 216 - Mathematical Methods for Biostatistics” (2024 Fall)
  • Neural Networks is a YouTube playlist by Grant Sanderson (3Blue1Brown); its chapters on gradient descent and backpropagation calculus visually illustrate how gradients of multivariate cost functions are computed and used for optimization.

## Further reading {#sec-additional-resources}

- @boyd2004convex
  is the standard reference on convex optimization, covering convex sets, convex functions, convex optimization problems, duality, unconstrained minimization, and interior-point methods.
- @nocedal2006numerical
  is a comprehensive reference on continuous numerical optimization, covering line search methods, trust-region methods, conjugate gradient methods, quasi-Newton methods, and nonlinear equations.
- [Hua Zhou](https://hua-zhou.github.io/)'s [course page for "UCLA Biostat 257 - Statistical Computing" (2025 Spring)](https://ucla-biostat-257.github.io/2025spring/)
  has lectures on optimization, from root finding to constrained problems.
  Its source is [on GitHub](https://github.com/ucla-biostat-257/2025spring) under the MIT License.
- [Hua Zhou](https://hua-zhou.github.io/)'s [lecture notes for "UCLA Biostat 216 - Mathematical Methods for Biostatistics" (2024 Fall)](https://ucla-biostat-216.github.io/2024fall/slides/13-optim/13-optim.html)
- [Neural Networks](https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLZHQObOWTQDNU6R1_67000Dx_ZCJB-3pi)
  is a YouTube playlist by Grant Sanderson (3Blue1Brown);
  its chapters on gradient descent and backpropagation calculus visually illustrate
  how gradients of multivariate cost functions are computed and used for optimization.
